Latent Multimodal Functional Graphical Model Estimation
Katherine Tsai, Boxin Zhao, Sanmi Koyejo, Mladen Kolar

Abstract
Joint multimodal functional data acquisition, where functional data from multiple modes are measured simultaneously from the same subject, has emerged as an exciting modern approach enabled by recent engineering breakthroughs in the neurological and biological sciences. One prominent motivation to acquire such data is to enable new discoveries of the underlying connectivity by combining multimodal signals. Despite the scientific interest, there remains a gap in principled statistical methods for estimating the graph underlying multimodal functional data. To this end, we propose a new integrative framework that models the data generation process and identifies operators mapping from the observation space to the latent space. We then develop an estimator that simultaneously estimates the transformation operators and the latent graph. This estimator is based on the partial correlation operator, which we rigorously extend from the multivariate to the functional setting. Our procedure is provably efficient, with the estimator converging to a stationary point with quantifiable statistical error. Furthermore, we show recovery of the latent graph under mild conditions. Our work is applied to analyze simultaneously acquired multimodal brain imaging data where the graph indicates functional connectivity of the brain. We present simulation and empirical results that support the benefits of joint estimation.



Keywords: integrative analysis; multimodal data; functional Gaussian graphical model; Neighborhood regression

1
Introduction

Recent engineering breakthroughs have enabled new ways to acquire rich multimodal data from individual subjects. For example, high-throughput sequencing enables the acquisition of genotype, and gene expression, among other signals, from the same set of subjects
(
Hao et al. 2021
)
. Our work is motivated by emerging technology that simultaneously acquires data from electroencephalogram (EEG) and functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI)
(
Morillon et al. 2010
)
. FMRI and EEG data are multivariate time series, where, after standard preprocessing
(
Wirsich et al. 2020b
)
, each dimension represents a region of the brain. We study the case where both the EEG and fMRI data are parcellated into the same atlas, resulting in the same number of dimensions. We view the time series of each region as a function of time, namely the functional data
(
Ramsay and Silverman 2005
)
, given the continuous underlying brain signals and the high sampling rate of the measurements. Our goal is to estimate the functional connectivity of the brain by solving a graph estimation problem
(
Qiu et al. 2016
;
Qiao et al. 2019
)
.

There are several challenges in integrating multimodal functional data for our problem.
First, estimation is often performed in a high-dimensional setting, where the ambient dimension is much larger than the sample size. Furthermore, multimodal data are often highly correlated across modes, since they measure related features of the same subject. Thus, many high-dimensional methods, such as graphical lasso
(
Yuan and Lin 2007
)
, are not easily applied, as they require restrictions on correlations to achieve the desired statistical properties. Furthermore, in our setting of interest, the EEG and fMRI data are noisy and confounded by non-neural activity
(
Murphy et al. 2013
;
Goto et al. 2016
)
. Therefore, the graphs estimated by either modality alone are inaccurate. Due to the observed structured noise and confounders, the graphs estimated separately from the two modalities may be dissimilar, although they contain partial information about the same underlying brain network
(
Wirsich et al. 2020a
)
. To address these shortcomings, we present a novel generative framework for observed processes (EEG-fMRI measurements) and latent processes (the underlying brain networks). Then, we provide a framework to jointly estimate the inverse operator mapping from the observed to the latent space, along with the latent graph, which encodes conditional independence.

Our framework uses functional graphical models
(
Qiao et al. 2019
;
Zhao et al. 2021
)
as a building block. We study the setting in which multimodal temporal data are viewed as functions belonging to different Hilbert spaces or subspaces of the same Hilbert space. This allows us to encode temporal characteristics using functional scores, a vector of real numbers obtained by projection to specific bases, effectively circumventing the need to address temporal discrepancies between modalities; for example, the bottom right of Figure
1
indicates that fMRI and EEG recordings have distinct frequency characteristics, belonging to different subspaces of the space of continuous functions. Our estimation approach handles such a challenging setting by first constructing linear operators that transform the functional data from the observation spaces to a shared latent space. Then, we estimate the latent graph using functional neighborhood regression. Our algorithm jointly estimates the graphical model and the linear operators through an alternating iterative procedure.

Our work offers several contributions.
From the modeling perspective, we propose an integrative function-on-function regression framework to estimate a latent functional Gaussian graphical model. We develop a novel initialization method inspired by the equivalence of the maximum log-likelihood estimator of the latent model and canonical correlation analysis
(
Bach and Jordan 2005
)
,

when there are two data modalities.
While our focus is on two data modalities, the proposed initialization method can be generalized to
M\geq 2
by merging the data modalities, as discussed in Appendix
J.3
.

Subsequently, we devise an efficient iterative method that boasts a linear rate of convergence. Specifically, our optimization analysis draws inspiration from recent advances in nonconvex optimization
(
Zhang et al. 2018
)
. Regarding the theoretical contributions, we demonstrate that, under mild conditions, we can recover the latent graph with high probability. We showcase the effectiveness of the model through simulations and the analysis of concurrent EEG-fMRI. Empirical results indicate improved prediction scores when using the estimated latent graph.

Figure 1:
An illustration of the generative processes.

Top left
: The latent graph of interest.
Bottom left
: The diagram of the generative model.
Top right
: Observed processes from multiple sources. Simultaneous fMRI recordings,
\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{i}(t)
, and EEG recordings,
\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{2}(t)
, from
Morillon et al. 2010
.

Bottom right
: The spectrum of fMRI dominates in the lower frequency domain while that of EEG spans to a higher frequency domain.

2
Methodology

This section introduces a generative model for multimodal data. As our goal is to estimate the latent graphical model using regression, we establish the relation between function-on-function regression and conditional independence in the Gaussian setting. We rigorously define the partial covariance operator and prove its equivalence to conditional independence.

2.1
Notation

Given a separable Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
of continuous functions with a Complete OrthoNormal System (CONS):
\{\phi_{\ell}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
, the inner product is defined as
\langle{f},{g}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\langle{f},{\phi_{i}}\rangle\langle{g},{\phi_{i}}\rangle
, where
\langle{f},{\phi_{i}}\rangle=\int_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}f(t)\phi_{i}(t)dt
. The induced norm is defined as
\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}=(\langle{f},{f}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}})^{1/2}
for any
f\in\mathbb{H}
. We let
\mathbb{H}^{p}
be the Cartesian product of
\mathbb{H}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{H}^{(p)}
where
\mathbb{H}^{(j)}\equiv\mathbb{H}
for all
j=1,\ldots,p
and its inner product is defined as
\langle{f},{g}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}^{p}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\langle{f_{i}},{g_{i}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
for any
f,g\in\mathbb{H}^{p}
. Let
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H}_{1},\mathbb{H}_{2})
be the class of linear bounded operators from
\mathbb{H}_{1}
to
\mathbb{H}_{2}
.
A linear operator
\mathscr{K}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H}_{1},\mathbb{H}_{2})
is in the equivalence class of a zero operator if
\mathscr{K}\in\{\mathscr{A}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H}_{1},\mathbb{H}_{2}):\langle{f},{\mathscr{K}g}\rangle=0,\;\forall g\in\mathbb{H}_{1},f\in\mathbb{H}_{2}\}
.
For any
\mathscr{T}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H}_{1},\mathbb{H}_{2})
, we define the Hilbert-Schmidt norm as
\left\|\mathscr{T}\right\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\|\mathscr{T}\phi_{1,\ell}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{2}}^{2}
, where
\{\phi_{1,\ell}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a CONS for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
|\!|\!|\mathscr{T}|\!|\!|=\sup_{f\in\mathbb{H}_{1},f\neq 0}\|\mathscr{T}f\|_{\mathbb{H}_{2}}/\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}_{1}}
.
\mathscr{T}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator if
\left\|\mathscr{T}\right\|_{\text{HS}}<\infty
. Given an operator
\mathscr{T}
,
\Image(\mathscr{T})
is the image space of
\mathscr{T}
.
Let
\chi
be a random element taking values in a measurable space
(\mathbb{H},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{H}))
, where
\mathscr{B}(\cdot)
denotes the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. A random element
\chi
in
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mu)
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\|\chi\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\int_{\Omega}\|\chi\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}d\mu<\infty
. Let
x_{1},y\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathbb{H}_{2}
, we define the tensor product
(x_{2}\otimes x_{1}):\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
as
(x_{2}\otimes x_{1})y=\langle{x_{1}},{y}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}_{1}}x_{2}
. The mean element is defined as
\mathfrak{m}=\int_{\Omega}\chi d\mu
and the covariance operator
\mathscr{K}\in\mathfrak{B}({\mathbb{H}},{\mathbb{H}})
for
\chi
is defined as
\mathscr{K}=\mathbb{E}[(\chi-\mathfrak{m})\otimes(\chi-\mathfrak{m})]=\int_{\Omega}(\chi-\mathfrak{m})\otimes(\chi-\mathfrak{m})d\mu
. Given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and for
p,q\geq 1
, we define
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{p,q}=(\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|a_{ij}|^{p})^{q/p})^{1/q}
,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{p}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\|_{p}/\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}
as the Frobenius norm of
\mathbf{A}
.
We use
\mathbb{N}
to denote positive integers.

2.2
The Latent and Observed Processes

Suppose that there are
M
modalities of functional data, each living in a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{m}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
. For modality
m
, we observe a
p
-dimensional random function
\mathcal{Y}^{m}=(\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{m},\ldots,\mathcal{Y}_{p}^{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{H}_{m}^{p}
, where
\mathbb{H}_{m}
is a separable Hilbert space of continuous functions defined on a closed interval
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Additionally, denote by
\mathbb{H}
the latent separable Hilbert space of continuous functions defined on the closed interval
\mathcal{T}
. Assume that
\mathcal{Y}^{m}
is driven by some
p
-dimensional latent processes
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{p})\in\mathbb{H}^{p}
where
\mathcal{Z}_{i}:L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)\rightarrow(\mathbb{H},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{H}))
is a centered Gaussian random element. In neuroscience applications, we have
M=2
modalities,
{\mathcal{Y}^{m}}
,
m=1,2
, which represent fMRI and EEG measurements, and
\mathcal{Z}
is the latent functional brain process.

Motivated by recent findings that EEG and fMRI measurements can be modeled as linear transformations of brain signals
(
Calhoun and Adali 2012
;
Chen et al. 2013
)
, we define
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}\in\mathfrak{B}({\mathbb{H}},{\mathbb{H}_{m}})
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, as data generation operators that transform data from latent space to observed spaces. We assume that the transformation operator is the same across nodes; hence
{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}
can be decomposed as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}=\chi_{i}^{m}+\xi_{i}^{m},\qquad\chi_{i}^{m}={\mathscr{L}^{m}}\mathcal{Z}_{i},\qquad i\in V,\;m=1,\ldots,M,

(2.1)

where
V=\{1,\ldots,p\}
is the set of vertices,
\chi_{i}^{m}
is obtained by a deterministic transformation of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in\mathbb{H}
,
the noise
\xi_{i}^{m}
, with
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}^{m}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\xi_{i}^{m}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{m}}^{2}]<\infty
, is the nuisance independent of
(\mathcal{Z}_{i})_{i\in V}
,
(\xi_{j}^{m})_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}
, and
(\xi_{j}^{m^{\prime}})_{m\neq m^{\prime},j=1,\ldots,p}
.
See Figure
1
for an illustration of the model.

To avoid identifiability issues,
we assume the following regularity conditions.

Assumption 1
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, be compact operators. The set of eigenfunctions associated with nonzero distinct eigenvalues, i.e.,
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\ldots
, of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{*}{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
is denoted as
\{\phi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{H}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
, where
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{*}
is the adjoint operator of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
. We assume that

z_{i,\ell}:=\langle{\mathcal{Z}_{i}},{\phi_{\ell}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{N},

(2.2)

and
z_{i,\ell}
is not correlated with
z_{i,\ell^{\prime}}
for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
.

This assumption is common in factor model analysis
(
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, Chapter 10.4)
. Note that for
i\neq j
,
z_{i,\ell}
and
z_{j,\ell^{\prime}}
may be correlated. The distinct eigenvalues assumption allows the set of eigenfunctions to be unique.

Let
V=\{1,\ldots,p\}
be the set of vertices, and let
E=\{(i,j):i,j\in V,i\neq j\}

be the set of edges. Our goal is to estimate the undirected latent graph
G=(V,E)
underlying
\mathcal{Z}
from the observed processes
\mathcal{Y}^{m}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
.
The conditional independence for functional graphical models is defined as in
Qiao et al. 2019
.

Definition 2.1
.

A centered Gaussian random vector
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{p}
follows a functional graphical model with respect to an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
if we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{j}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}\text{ if and only if }(i,j)\not\in E,

where
\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}=\{\mathcal{Z}_{j^{\prime}}:j^{\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}\}
denotes the components of
\mathcal{Z}
indexed by
V\backslash\{i,j\}
.

Qiao et al. 2019
estimate
E
by studying the inverse covariance operator of
\mathcal{Z}
, which generally does not exist in infinite dimensional space and can only be well-approximated under some restrictions on the eigenvalues of the covariance operator. In contrast, in the multivariate Gaussian setting, it is well known that conditional covariance is zero if and only if the partial covariance is zero, which motivates neighborhood regression as an estimator of conditional independence
(
Meinshausen and Bühlmann 2006
;
Peng et al. 2009
)
. We develop a neighborhood regression estimator for the functional data setting, bypassing the need to compute the inverse covariance operator directly.

2.3
Functional Partial Covariance Operator

Although the relation between the partial and conditional correlation has been implicitly stated in the RKHS setting
(
Fukumizu et al. 2009
)
, to our knowledge, the formal notion of partial covariance operators has not been clearly established in the literature on functional Gaussian graphical models. The form of the regression model that establishes the equivalence of conditional independence is unclear: when does a zero
regression element
imply conditional independence? To answer this, we introduce the partial covariance operator and establish the corresponding equivalence with respect to conditional independence.

The regression of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
on
\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}
, is defined as

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{\beta}_{ij^{\prime}}\}_{j^{\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{ij^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\\
j^{\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg\|\mathcal{Z}_{i}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}}\beta_{ij^{\prime}}\mathcal{Z}_{j^{\prime}}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\right],

(2.3)

where
\beta_{ij}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
is a bounded linear operator.
For any
f,g\in\mathbb{H}
, we define the partial cross covariance operator
\mathscr{K}_{ij\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}}
as:

\left\langle{\mathscr{K}_{ij\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}}f},{g}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle{\mathcal{Z}_{i}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}}\widetilde{\beta}_{ij^{\prime}}\mathcal{Z}_{j^{\prime}}},{g}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\left\langle{\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\sum_{j^{\prime\prime}\in V\backslash\{i,j\}}\widetilde{\beta}_{jj^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{Z}_{j^{\prime\prime}}},{f}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right],

(2.4)

that is, the cross covariance operator of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
after removing the effect of
\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}
.
The partial cross covariance operator
\mathscr{K}_{ij\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}}
is a bounded linear operator, which can be shown using the same technique as in Lemma
A.7
.
We show the following result.

Theorem 2.2
.

The partial cross covariance operators

\mathscr{K}_{ij\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}}
and
\mathscr{K}_{ji\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}}
are in the equivalence class of the zero operator if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{j}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}
or, equivalently, if
(i,j)\not\in E
.

This result allows us to estimate the partial covariance operator to measure the conditional independence of two nodes given the remaining nodes. An immediate corollary to Theorem
2.2
is that we can write
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
as a linear combination of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
,
j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}
.

Corollary 2.3
.

There exists
\beta_{ij}^{\star}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
,
j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\beta_{ij}^{\star}\mathcal{Z}_{j}+\mathcal{W}_{i},

(2.5)

and
\mathcal{W}_{i}\in\mathbb{H}
is a Gaussian random function independent of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
,
j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}
. Furthermore,
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
is in the equivalence class of the zero operator if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{j}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}
.

Based on Corollary
2.3
, specifically the relationship (
2.5
), we can apply functional regression to measure conditional independence. However, since the space of bounded linear operators
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
is infinite dimensional, it is computationally intractable to estimate the parameters in (
2.5
). Thus, it is important to study a subclass of models that can be well-approximated by finite-rank operators. As a consequence, we study the case where the true regression operators
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
are compact and
\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{\text{HS}}<\infty
.

Assumption 2
.

The operators
\beta^{\star}_{ij}
,
i,j\in V
and
i\neq j
, are Hilbert-Schmidt operators.

Our formulation of functional neighborhood regression closely follows the formulation in
Zhao et al. 2021
. Since
\beta_{ij}^{\star}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\text{HS}}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, it admits a singular system
(
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, Theorem 4.3.1)
. Hence, we are able to represent the singular system in terms of
\{\phi_{\ell},\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
and obtain

\beta_{ij}^{\star}=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime}},\quad i,j\in V,i\neq j,

(2.6)

where
b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}
. Under Theorem
2.2
, one can verify that
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{j}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{-(i,j)}
if and only if
\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{HS}=0
. Therefore, the set of neighbors of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}

is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}=\{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}:\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{HS}>0\}.

(2.7)

In the high-dimensional setting, where
p
is larger than the sample size
N
, we often consider that the coefficients are sparse
(
Meinshausen and Bühlmann 2006
)
, rendering a sparse network structure. Such structure is often observed in biological and clinical experiments
(
Tsai et al. 2022
)
. In the functional data scenario, a sparse functional network is equivalent to assuming that many of
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
are zero operators:

Assumption 3
.

The set
\{\beta_{ij}^{\star}\}_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}
has at most
s^{\star}
nonzero operators.

Assumption
3
implies that
the neighborhood
\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}
, defined in (
2.7
), satisfies

\left|\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}\right|\leq s^{\star}
.

For
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
not a zero operator, we expect that
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
can be well approximated by some finite-rank operator: consider a
k
-dimensional subspace of the original space, denoted as
\{\phi_{\ell}:\ell=1,\ldots,k\}
, we want
\beta_{ij}^{k\star}:=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{k}\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}\approx\beta_{ij}^{\star}
.
Observe that
\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{HS}=\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}-\beta_{ij}^{\star{k}}+\beta_{ij}^{\star{k}}\|_{HS}\leq\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}-\beta_{ij}^{\star{k}}\|_{HS}+\|\beta_{ij}^{\star{k}}\|_{HS}
.
Hence, if there exists a positive
\epsilon_{0}
such that
\min_{i\in V}\min_{j\in\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}}\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{HS}\geq 2\epsilon_{0}
,
then we can choose a
{k}
such that the truncated signal still has half the magnitude of the original signal:
\|\beta_{ij}^{{k\star}}\|_{HS}\geq\epsilon_{0}
. We define

\Lambda(k):=\min_{i\in V}\min_{j\in\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}}\|\beta_{ij}^{k\star}\|_{HS}.

In addition, for
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
not a zero operator, we expect that most of
b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}
,
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, are zero or have a small magnitude. We make the following assumption on
\beta^{\star}_{ij}
.

Assumption 4
.

The rank
k
is selected such that
\Lambda(k)\geq(1/2)\min_{i\in V}\min_{j\in\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}}\|\beta_{ij}^{\star}\|_{HS}=:\epsilon_{0}
.
There exists a constant
\alpha^{\star}\in(0,1]
such that the sets
\{b_{ij,\ell 1}^{\star},\ldots,b_{ij,\ell k}^{\star}\}
and
\{b_{ij,1\ell^{\prime}}^{\star},\ldots,b_{ij,k\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\}
each have at most
\alpha^{\star}k
non-zero coefficients, for
\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k
.

The first assumption implies that (
2.7
) is equivalent as

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}=\{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}:\|\beta_{ij}^{k\star}\|_{HS}>\epsilon_{0}\}.

(2.8)

The second structural assumption implies that each basis function is only correlated with at most
\alpha k-1
other basis functions but we allow the rank to grow with
k
. From the neuroscience perspective
(
Olsen et al. 2012
;
Vidaurre et al. 2017
)
, the first level of sparsity, Assumption
3
, corresponds to the sparse connectivity between sub-networks of the whole connectome.

We consider
\phi_{\ell}
to be the
\ell
-th cognitive process
(
Posner et al. 1988
)
, e.g., visual imaging, word reading, shifting visual attention, and etc. Assumption 4 implies two connectivity structures, as outlined in the following. First, each node
i
only has a few ongoing cognitive processes. Secondly, each connectivity process
\ell
in node
i
is sparsely correlated with the other cognitive process
\ell^{\prime}
in node
j

(
Park and Friston 2013
;
Vidaurre et al. 2017
)
. This assumption further implies that the operator
\beta_{ij}^{\star}
is approximately low-rank, because many of the
\{b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\}
are zero.
Additionally, the variability of sparsity patterns imposed by Assumption 3–4 are subject to the cognitive tasks performed. For example,
Cohen and D’Esposito 2016
have found that during the motor cognitive process, within sub-network connectivity is highly activated, meaning that
\alpha^{\star}
may be high. In contrast, during the memory task, between-network communication is more active, meaning that
s^{\star}
might be high.

While the former assumption is well recognized in functional graphical model literature
(
Qiao et al. 2019
)
, we argue that adding the second level sparsity assumption provides more informative interpretability of the underlying functional processes.

3
Estimation

We describe the estimation of the parameters for the generative model described in Section
2
. We first introduce an estimation procedure for the transformation operator, which we formulate as an inverse problem. Then, the objective function in infinite dimensional space is outlined in Section
3.2
. Finally, the objective function in a finite dimensional space along with the structured assumptions are discussed in Section
3.3
.

3.1
Transformations to the Latent Space

Our problem is to estimate the graphical model
G=(V,E)
in the latent space through data from multiple modalities
\mathcal{Y}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Y}^{M}
. While the mathematical formulation in (
2.1
) outlines the generative model, from the estimation point of view, we focus on its inverse, that is, the linear operators that transform the functional processes from the observation spaces to the latent space. In general, the inverse operator of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
does not exist; even if it does exist, it might not be continuous. However, the inverse operator is well defined when the operator domain is restricted to
{\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})}
, giving us hope to recover the inverse of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
on the restricted domain.
Following Definition 3.5.7 in
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, we define the Moore-Penrose (generalized) inverse of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{m}
be the operator
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
restricted to
\ker({\mathscr{L}^{m}})^{\perp}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
.
The domain of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger}
is defined as
\Domain({\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger})=\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})\oplus\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})^{\perp}
. Then, for any
y\in\Domain({\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger})
,
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger}y=(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{m})^{-1}y
if
y\in\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})
and
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger}y=0
if
y\in\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})^{\perp}
.

We use
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
to denote the pseudoinverse operator
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}^{\dagger}
for simplicity of notation.
Since
{\chi_{i}^{m}}\in\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})
, we have the following relationship:

\mathcal{Z}_{i}={\mathscr{A}^{m}}(\chi_{i}^{m}),\qquad m=1,\ldots,M,\;i\in V.

(3.1)

The operator
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
maps the random elements from the observation spaces back to the latent space, where we can jointly estimate the latent graph from multiple views
\mathcal{Y}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Y}^{M}
.

The following representation of
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
will be useful for estimation from the observed data. Let
\{\phi_{\ell}^{m}\in\mathbb{H}_{m}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
be the set of eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{L}^{m}\mathscr{L}^{m*}
. Since
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}\in\mathfrak{B}({\mathbb{H}},{\mathbb{H}_{m}})
is a compact operator, when the domain is restricted to
\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})\oplus\Image({\mathscr{L}^{m}})^{\perp}
we have:

{\mathscr{A}^{m}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m\star}\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m},\quad{\mathscr{A}^{m}}\chi=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m\star}\langle{\chi},{\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}_{m}}\phi_{\ell},

(3.2)

where
a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m\star}\in\mathbb{R}
are coefficients and
\chi\in\Domain({\mathscr{A}^{m}})
.

Although
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
is well defined on the restricted domain, it is not necessarily a bounded operator. Since
\mathscr{L}^{m}
is a compact operator, to have a bounded inverse
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
,
\mathscr{L}^{m}
must be finite dimensional
(
Groetsch 1984
, Corollary 1.3.3)
. The assumption is mild in our application because the rank of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
can be arbitrarily large, so long as it is finite.

3.2
Approximation of Infinite Dimensional Estimator

When the noise
{\xi_{i}^{m}}
in (
2.1
) is small in magnitude compared to
{\chi_{i}^{m}}
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{i}={\mathscr{A}^{m}}{\chi_{i}^{m}}\approx{\mathscr{A}^{m}}{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}
. We combine (
2.3
) and the estimation of
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
and optimize the objective:

\min_{\{{\mathscr{A}^{m}}\},\{\beta_{ij}\}}\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg\|{\mathscr{A}^{m}}{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}-\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\beta_{ij}{\mathscr{A}^{m}}\mathcal{Y}_{m,j}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\right],

(3.3)

where
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
is of the form (
3.2
) and
\beta_{ij}
is of the form (
2.6
).

The temporal realization of random elements
\mathcal{Z}_{i}(\omega,\cdot)
and
{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}(\omega,\cdot)
,
\omega\in\Omega
, are bivariate functions
\mathcal{Z}_{i}(\omega,t)
and
{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}(\omega,t)
that we assume are jointly measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}\times\mathscr{B}({\mathcal{T}})
.
Since both
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}
are mean-squared integrable random elements taking values in spaces of continuous functions, by the Karhunen–Loève theorem
(
Karhunen 1947
;
Loève 1945
)
, we can uniquely represent them as

\mathcal{Z}_{i}(\omega,t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}z_{i,\ell}(\omega)\phi_{\ell}(t),\qquad{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}(\omega,t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}y_{i,\ell}^{m}(\omega)\phi_{\ell}^{m}(t),\qquad\omega\in\Omega,\;t\in{\mathcal{T}},

(3.4)

where we recall that
z_{i,\ell}(\omega)
is defined in (
2.2
) and
y_{i,\ell}^{m}(\omega)=\langle{{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}(\omega,\cdot)},{\phi_{\ell}^{m}(\cdot)}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}_{m}}
.
For conciseness, we omit
\omega
from the notation on
y_{i,\ell}^{m}(\omega)
and
z_{i,\ell}(\omega)
, and
t
on
\phi_{\ell}(t)
and
\phi_{\ell}^{m}(t)
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The optimization problem in (
3.3
) is equivalent to

\min_{\{a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m}\},\{b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}\}}\qquad\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\sum_{\ell^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}a_{\ell\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{m}y_{i,\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{m}-\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}a_{\ell^{\prime}\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{m}y_{j,\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{m}\right)^{2}\right],

(3.5)

where
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m}\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m}
and
\beta_{ij}=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime\prime}}b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime\prime}}\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
.

Based on Proposition
3.2
, the optimization over the operators
{\mathscr{A}^{m}}
and
\beta_{ij}
in (
3.3
) is transformed to optimization over sequences of real numbers
\{a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m}\}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
and
\{b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}\}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}
for
i\in V
and
j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}
.
While functional data live in infinite dimensional spaces, in practice, one is faced with finite computational resources and is forced to truncate both the latent functions and observed functions to perform estimation. In addition, Assumption
3
–
4
indicates that most of
b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}
are zero.
Hence, using the result from (
3.4
), the
{k}
-dimensional approximation of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
via projection to
\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{k}\}
is
\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}z_{i,\ell}\phi_{\ell}
.
For the observed space, we assume that the signals are smooth enough such that the
k_{m}
-dimensional projection
\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{m}}y_{i,\ell}^{m}\phi_{\ell}^{m}
can well approximate
{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m}}
. These observations imply that the following optimization problem can recover the model parameters sufficiently well:

\mathbf{A}^{\star},\mathbf{B}^{\star}:=\arg\min_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m}-\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\mathbf{B}_{ij}{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\mathbf{y}_{j}^{m}\bigg\|_{F}^{2}\right],

(3.6)

where
\mathbf{A}^{\star}=\{\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\}_{m=1}^{M}
,

\mathbf{B}^{\star}=\{\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\}_{i=1}^{p}
,

\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m}=(y_{i,1}^{m},\ldots,y_{i,k_{m}}^{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}}
,

{\mathbf{A}^{m}}=(a_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m})_{\ell=1,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k,k_{m}}
, and

\mathbf{B}_{ij}=(b_{ij,\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{\ell=1,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. The parameters
k
and
k_{m}
are suitably chosen – we discuss the selection of
k
and
k_{m}
in more detail in Section
4.2
.

3.3
Empirical Objective Function and Structured Assumptions

Throughout the section, we assume the sets of basis
\{\phi_{1}^{m},\ldots,\phi_{k_{m}}^{m}\}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
are provided. We are given independent observations from
N
individuals, where for each individual
n
and each vertex
i
, we have data from
M
modalities,
\{\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m,(n)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
. The vector
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m,(n)}
is obtained by projecting
\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{m,(n)}
onto the set of basis
\{\phi_{1}^{m},\ldots,\phi_{k_{m}}^{m}\}
, and we let
{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{m}}=(\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m,(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m,(N)})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}\times N}
.
The sample version of the objective in (
3.6
) is:

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{2N}\bigg\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{m}}-\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\mathbf{B}_{ij}{\mathbf{A}^{m}}{\mathbf{Y}_{j}^{m}}\bigg\|_{F}^{2}.

(3.7)

To enforce the sparsity of
\mathbf{B}
, we define the following norm.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{U}=(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{U}_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times kp}
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
.
We define the norm

\|\mathbf{U}\|_{r(k,\ell)}=\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\VEC\left(\mathbf{U}_{1}\right)\cdots\VEC\left(\mathbf{U}_{p}\right)\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{2,\ell},\quad\ell\geq 1,

and let
\|\mathbf{U}\|_{r(k,0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbbm{1}(\|\VEC\left(\mathbf{U}_{i}\right)\|_{2}>0)
, where
\mathbbm{1}(\cdot)
denotes the indicator function.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}=(\mathbf{B}_{i1}^{\star}\cdots\mathbf{B}_{i(i-1)}^{\star}\mathbf{B}_{i(i+1)}^{\star}\cdots\mathbf{B}_{ip}^{\star})\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k(p-1)}
.
Under Assumption
3
, we have that
\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{r(k,0)}\leq s^{\star}
.
Assumption
4
implies that each nonzero submatrix
\mathbf{B}_{ij}^{\star}
of
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}
has columns and rows with at most
\alpha
-fraction of nonzero entries. We define the following constraint set

\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)=\{\mathbf{U}:\|\mathbf{U}\|_{r(k,0)}\leq s,\|\mathbf{U}_{j,m\cdot}\|_{0},\|\mathbf{U}_{j,\cdot n}\|_{0}\leq\alpha k,m,n=1,\ldots,k,j=1,\ldots,p-1\},

where
\mathbf{U}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
denotes the sub-matrix of
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k(p-1)}
.
We assume that
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k_{m}}
satisfies the following constraints. Let
\mathbf{W}_{j\cdot}
be the
j
-th row of a matrix
\mathbf{W}
, we define

\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\left\{\mathbf{W}:\tau_{1}^{1/2}\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A}^{m(0)})\leq\|\mathbf{W}_{j\cdot}\|_{2}\leq(\tau_{2}/k_{m})^{1/2}\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}),\;j=1,\ldots k\right\},

(3.8)

where
\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}
is the initial guess.
We will later verify that there exist some
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}>0
such that
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
lies in
\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
.
The lower bound
\tau_{1}
ensures that any row of
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}

is always bounded away from zero.
1
1

1

We need to make sure that
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
is nonzero so that the solution is nontrivial, since
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}={\bf 0}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}={\bf 0}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
and
i\in V
is always an optimal solution in the unconstrained optimization problem.

If
\tau_{2}
is proportional to
k^{1/2}
, then the upper bound constraint can be viewed as the incoherence condition
(
Candès et al. 2011
)
.
We want to optimize the following objective function:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle\quad f(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha),\;i\in V;

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\;{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),\;m=1,\ldots,M.

4
Algorithm

Algorithm 1

Latent Graph Estimation

Input:
\{\mathbf{Y}_{m}\}_{m=1,\ldots,M}
,
\{\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}\}_{m=1,\ldots,M}
,
\{\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}\}_{i\in V}
,
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
,
s
,
\alpha
,
\eta_{A}
,
\eta_{B}
;

Output:
\{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{m}\}_{m=1,\ldots,M}
,
\{\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{i}\}_{i\in V}

while
Not converged
do

for
m=1,…,M
do

\mathbf{A}^{m(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{m,\tau}(\mathbf{A}^{m(t)}-\eta_{A}\nabla_{{\mathbf{A}^{m}}}f
)

for

i\in V

do

\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(t+.5)}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{s}(\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(t)}-\eta_{B}\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{i}}f
)

for

j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}

do

\mathbf{B}_{ij}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{ij}^{(t+.5)})

We propose a two-stage algorithm to minimize (
3.9
) efficiently.
We start by introducing the second stage.
Given a suitably chosen initial tuple
(\mathbf{A}^{(0)},\mathbf{B}^{(0)})
from the first stage,
we use the alternating projected gradient descent with the group-sparse hard-thresholding operator. We first define the
s
-group sparse truncation operator as

\displaystyle[{\mathcal{T}}_{s}(\mathbf{B}_{i})]_{\cdot,k(j-1)+1:kj}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{B}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},&\text{the magnitude $\|\mathbf{B}_{ij}\|_{F}$ is among the top-s of $\{\mathbf{B}_{ij}\}$};\\
{\bf 0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

The operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
keeps the largest
s
submatrices of
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k(p-1)}
.
Next, we define the
\alpha
-truncation operator as

[\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{ij})]_{u,v}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}[\mathbf{B}_{ij}]_{u,v},&\text{$[\mathbf{B}_{ij}]_{u,v}$ is one of the $\alpha k$ largest elements in}\\
&\text{magnitude of $[\mathbf{B}_{ij}]_{u,\cdot}$ and $[\mathbf{B}_{ij}]_{\cdot,v}$.}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

The operator
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}
keeps the largest
\alpha
-fraction of entries in each row and column of
\mathbf{B}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.
Then, the projection to the set
\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)
can be implemented by the composition of
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}
, as shown in Algorithm
1
. The operator
\mathcal{P}_{m,\tau}
as the projection operator to the set
\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m,\tau}({\mathbf{A}^{m}})=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\|\mathbf{W}-{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\|_{F}.

After Algorithm
1
converges, we select the edges of the graph either using the AND or OR operation with a threshold
\epsilon_{0}>0
:

\widehat{\mathscr{N}}_{i}(\epsilon_{0})=\{(i,j):\|\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{ij}\|_{F}\geq\epsilon_{0}\text{ and (or) }\|\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{ji}\|_{F}\geq\epsilon_{0}\}.

(4.1)

4.1
Initialization Procedure

We describe a procedure to find a good initial tuple
(\{\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}\}_{m=1}^{M},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}\}_{i=1}^{p})
.
We focus on the case when
M=2
, as this is the case for the motivating example.
To find initial estimates, we connect the problem with probabilistic canonical correlation analysis.
We first construct
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
by finding the canonical correlation between two views and then compute
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}
with an iterative method by fixing
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
.

Let
\mathbf{L}^{m\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}\times k}
be such that the
\ell\ell^{\prime}
-th entry of
\mathbf{L}^{m\star}
is
L_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{m\star}=\langle{{\mathscr{L}^{m}}\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m}},{\phi_{\ell}^{m}}\rangle
. Let
{\mathbf{q}}_{i}^{m}=(q_{i,1}^{m},\ldots,q_{i,k_{m}}^{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}}
, where
q_{i,j}^{m}=\langle{\xi_{i}^{m}},{\phi_{j}^{m}}\rangle
, be the truncation of
\xi_{i}^{m}
;
{\mu}_{i}^{m}=(\mu_{i,1}^{m},\ldots,\mu_{i,k_{m}}^{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}}
be the bias induced by finite-dimensional truncation and
\mu_{i,\ell^{\prime}}^{m}=\sum_{\ell=k+1}^{\infty}\langle{{\mathscr{L}^{m}}(z_{i,\ell}\phi_{\ell})},{\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m}}\rangle
for
\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k_{m}
. Recalling that
\mathbf{z}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bf 0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{i}^{m}|\mathbf{z}_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L}^{m\star}\mathbf{z}_{i},\bm{\Sigma}^{m,\mathbf{q}}_{i,i}+\bm{\Sigma}^{m,{\bm{\mu}}}_{i,i}),

(4.2)

where
\bm{\Sigma}^{m,{\bm{\mu}}}_{i,i}
is the covariance of
\mu_{i}^{m}
and
\bm{\Sigma}^{m,{\mathbf{q}}}_{i}
is the covariance of
\mathbf{q}_{i}^{m}
. From (
2.2
) we have that
\bm{\mu}_{i}^{m}
is uncorrelated with
\mathbf{z}_{i}
. Recall that we assume that
\mathbf{L}^{m\star}
is the same across
i\in V
.
We briefly discuss how extend to the setting where
\mathbf{L}_{i}^{m\star}
differ across the nodes in Appendix
J.2
.
To estimate
\mathbf{L}^{m\star}
from data, we pick the first node
i=1
for convenience.
We discuss an alternative to this strategy in Appendix
J.1

The log-likelihood is defined as

\displaystyle\ell({\bf S})=(k_{1}+k_{2})\log 2\pi

\displaystyle+\log\left|{\bf S}\right|+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}({\bf S}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}),

\displaystyle{\bf S}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{L}^{1}{\mathbf{L}^{1}}^{\top}+\bm{\Sigma}^{1,\mathbf{q}}_{11}&\mathbf{L}^{1}{\mathbf{L}^{2}}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{L}^{2}{\mathbf{L}^{1}}^{\top}&\mathbf{L}^{2}{\mathbf{L}^{2}}^{\top}+\bm{\Sigma}^{2,\mathbf{q}}_{11}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}_{1}^{1,(n)}\mathbf{y}_{1}^{1,(n)\top}&\mathbf{y}_{1}^{1,(n)}\mathbf{y}_{1}^{2,(n)\top}\\
\mathbf{y}_{1}^{2,(n)}\mathbf{y}_{1}^{1,(n)\top}&\mathbf{y}_{1}^{2,(n)}\mathbf{y}_{1}^{2,(n)\top}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\bm{\Sigma}^{m}_{11}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{m}_{11}
be the covariance matrix and sample covariance of node
1
for modality
m
, respectively. Let
\bm{\Sigma}^{12}_{11}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{12}_{11}
be the cross-covariance matrix and sample cross-covariance matrix for two modalities at node
1
, respectively. By Theorem 2 in
Bach and Jordan 2005
, the maximum log-likelihood estimator for
\mathbf{L}^{m\star}
is

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}^{m}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{m}_{11}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{m}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{1/2}
, where

\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are the top-k singular values of
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{12}=(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1}_{11})^{-1/2}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{12}_{11}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}_{11})^{-1/2}
, columns of
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{1}
are the corresponding top-k left singular vectors and columns of
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{2}
are the corresponding right singular vectors. The initial estimate
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
is obtained by taking the pseudo-inverse of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}^{m}
, denoted as

\displaystyle{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}=\bm{\Gamma}^{-1/2}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{m\top}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{m}_{11})^{-1/2}.

(4.3)

After obtaining
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
, we compute the initial estimate for
\mathbf{B}:=\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
. We solve the following constrained optimization problem:

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)\end{subarray}}h_{i}(\mathbf{B}_{i})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)\end{subarray}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{2MN}\bigg\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{m}}-\mathbf{B}_{i}\{\mathbf{I}_{p-1}\otimes{({\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)})}\}{\mathbf{Y}_{\backslash\{i\}}^{m}}\bigg\|_{F}^{2}

using the projected gradient descent described in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

Initialization of
\mathbf{B}

Input:
\{\mathbf{Y}_{m}\}_{m=1,\ldots,M}
,
\{\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}\}_{m=1,\ldots,M}
,
\{\mathbf{B}_{0,i}={\bf 0}_{i\in V}\}
,
s
,
\alpha
,
\eta_{B_{0}}
;

for

i\in V
,
t=1,\ldots,L-1

do

\mathbf{B}_{0,i}^{(t+.5)}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{s}(\mathbf{B}_{0,i}^{(t)}-\eta_{B_{0}}\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{0,i}}h)

for

j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}

do

\mathbf{B}_{0,ij}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{0,ij}^{(t+.5)})

\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}\leftarrow\mathbf{B}_{0,i}^{(L)}

4.2
Selection of Basis Parameters

We select
k_{m}
based on the projection score to the basis, where we employ the elbow method to decide
k_{m}
. Specifically, we compute the mean-squared error of the projected signals with the original signals and pick the elbow point. Then, we compute the canonical correlation of two views and select
k
based on the canonical correlation score using the elbow method.

Although Theorem
5.4
requires knowledge of
\varepsilon_{0}
, in practice we find setting
\varepsilon_{0}
to be a small constant
10^{-3}
suffices to give stable and reproducible results in various simulations and real data
.
The parameters
s
,
\alpha
,
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
are selected based on the
5
-fold cross-validation with the BIC score function used in the functional regression
(
Zhao et al. 2021
)
:

\displaystyle\BIC(s,\alpha,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\{\sum_{m=1}^{M}N\log\det\left(\frac{1}{2N}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{m}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{m\top}\right)+|\widehat{\mathscr{N}}_{i}(s,\alpha,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})|\log N\right\},

where
\mathbf{G}_{i}^{m}={\mathbf{A}^{m}}{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{m}}-\sum_{j\in{V\backslash\{i\}}}\mathbf{B}_{ij}{\mathbf{A}^{m}}{\mathbf{Y}_{j}^{m}}
for
i\in V
and
m=1,\ldots,M
.

5
Theory

We show that Algorithm
1
–
2
can recover the underlying latent graphs with high probability under mild conditions.
The quality of the graph estimate depends on how well we can estimate
\mathbf{A}^{\star}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
.
We start by showing the convergence guarantee for both parameters.
The convergence analysis provides the rate of the algorithmic convergence, along with the statistical error at the stationary points.

In Section
5.1
, we assess the quality of the initial estimates. The theory suggests the consistency of the estimator with a small truncation error. The convergence guarantee of Algorithm
1
is provided in Section
5.2
along with the quantification of the statistical error at the convergence points. Section
5.3
presents the guarantee of latent graph recovery.

5.1
Analysis of the Initialization

We quantify the initial error of
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
to
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}
to
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}
in terms of the sample size
N
. When the truncation error is negligible, the result suggests that
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
converges in Frobenius norm at a rate
O(\sqrt{k(k_{1}+k_{2})/N})
. Furthermore, we show that
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}
converges in Frobenius norm at a rate
O(\sqrt{k(k_{1}+k_{2})/N}+\sqrt{s^{\star}k^{2}/N})
, where the first term is induced by the estimation error of
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
and the second term is is the statistical error of
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}
.

We make the following assumption on the matrix
\mathbf{R}^{12}=(\bm{\Sigma}^{1}_{1,1})^{-1/2}\bm{\Sigma}^{12}_{1,1}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}_{1,1})^{-1/2}
.

Assumption 5
.

Suppose that
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\geq k
and the top-k singular values of
\mathbf{R}^{12}
satisfy:
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2}>\ldots>\gamma_{k}>0
.

By definition, the singular values of
\mathbf{R}^{12}
are the canonical correlations of two views. Assumption
5
is for identifiability purpose. It implies that the canonical vectors are unique, up to sign changes.
Let the sets of left and right singular vectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{12}
and
\mathbf{R}^{12}
be
\{(\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{1},\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{2})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k}
and
\{({\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{1\star},{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{2\star})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k}
, respectively. Hence we define
\mathbf{Q}
as a diagonal matrix whose
i
-th diagonal entry
q_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
satisfies the condition that
\langle{q_{i}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}^{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{i}^{1\star}}\rangle\geq 0
.

Assumption 6
.

The covariance
\bm{\Sigma}^{m}=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}^{m}\mathbf{Y}^{m\top}]\in\mathbb{R}^{pk_{m}\times pk_{m}}
satisfies

0<\nu_{x}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma}^{m})<\sigma_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma}^{m})\leq\rho_{x}<\infty,\quad m=1,\ldots,M.

We now establish the theory of the distance of
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
under sign matrix
\mathbf{Q}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\kappa_{m}=\sigma_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma}^{m})/\sigma_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma}^{m})
. Suppose that
N=O(\max_{m=1,2}\kappa_{m}^{2}k_{m})
and Assumption
5
–
6
hold. Let
\mathscr{L}^{m,r}=\sum_{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\in I}\langle{\mathscr{L}^{m}\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}},{\phi_{\ell}^{m}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}_{m}}\phi_{\ell}^{m}\otimes\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}
, with
I=\{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}:(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\neq(a,b),a=1,\ldots,k_{m},b=1,\ldots,k\}
, denote the truncation term. Then

\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}-\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}\leq C_{\gamma_{k},\nu,\rho}\left(1+\max_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{|\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}|}\right)\sqrt{k\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{N}}+\sqrt{8k}|\!|\!|\mathscr{A}^{m}|\!|\!|^{2}|\!|\!|\mathscr{L}^{m,r}|\!|\!|,

(5.1)

with probability at least
1-5\exp(-\sum_{m=1}^{2}k_{m})
, where

C_{\gamma_{k},\nu,\rho}>0
is a constant depending on
\gamma_{k}
,
\nu_{x}
, and
\rho_{x}
defined in Appendix
C
.

Remark 1
.

The second term in (
5.1
) is the truncation error, which becomes small as
k
and
k_{m}
grow. To obtain a non-trivial upper bound, we must have
|\!|\!|{\mathscr{A}^{m}}|\!|\!|<\infty
. Therefore, the smallest non-zero singular value of
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
should be bounded away from zero, suggesting that assuming
{\mathscr{L}^{m}}
to be a finite-rank operator
(
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, Theorem 4.2.3)
is a necessary condition.

If the truncation error scales the same as the statistical error, the first term of (
5.1
), the theorem tells us that
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
is reasonably close to
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
, up to a sign change, at the rate of
O(\sqrt{k(k_{1}+k_{2})/N})
.
We can achieve this by choosing the appropriate
k_{m}
because
|\!|\!|\mathscr{L}^{m,r}|\!|\!|
decreases with increasing
k_{m}
. Therefore, there exists a
k_{m}
such that
|\!|\!|\mathscr{L}^{m,r}|\!|\!|=O(\sqrt{(k_{1}+k_{2})/N})
.
Additionally, the first term of (
5.1
) is inversely proportional to the difference between two consecutive canonical correlations. Hence, the canonical correlations must be distinct, as stated in Assumption
5
, and a gap between two consecutive canonical correlations is required to obtain a non-trivial upper bound.

Note that multiplications with a signed diagonal matrix do not break structural assumptions; that is,
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

and
(\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{B}_{i1}^{\star}\mathbf{Q}\cdots\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{B}_{ip}^{\star}\mathbf{Q}){\top}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)
.
Furthermore, we have
f(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=f(\{\mathbf{Q}{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\},\{\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{B}_{i}(\mathbf{I}_{p-1}\otimes\mathbf{Q}^{\top})\})
and
h_{i}(\mathbf{B}_{i})=h_{i}(\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{B}_{i}(\mathbf{I}_{p-1}\otimes\mathbf{Q}^{\top}))
for any
\mathbf{Q}
. Therefore, it would be cumbersome to write out
\mathbf{Q}
explicitly for the rest of the analysis. We abuse the notation here by writing
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
as
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
and
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\mathbf{Q}^{\top}
as
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}
.
Define
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i}^{\star}=(-\mathbf{B}_{i1}^{\star}\cdots-\mathbf{B}_{ip}^{\star})\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times pk}
, where
\mathbf{B}_{ii}^{\star}=\mathbf{I}_{k}
.

Assumption 7
.

For every
i\in V
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i}^{\star}
satisfies

0<\nu_{b}^{1/2}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i}^{\star})<\sigma_{\max}(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i}^{\star})\leq\rho_{b}^{1/2}<\infty.

For every
m=1,\ldots,M
,
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
satisfies

0<\nu_{a}^{1/2}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A}^{m\star})<\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A}^{m\star})\leq\rho_{a}^{1/2}<\infty.

Assumptions
6
–
7
guarantee that
f(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}^{\star})
is strongly convex and smooth with respect to
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, and
f(\mathbf{A}^{\star},\mathbf{B})
is strongly convex and smooth with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{i}
,
i\in V
. Furthermore, combined with the result of Theorem
5.1
, it follows that
h_{i}(\mathbf{B})
is strongly convex and smooth with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{i}
for
i\in V
with high probability.

Let
s=\vartheta_{1}s^{\star}/2
,
\alpha=\vartheta_{2}\alpha^{\star}/2
for some constants
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
. Our theory requires more stringent conditions:
\vartheta_{1}
,
\vartheta_{2}
are some constants greater than
2
, which implies that
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}(s^{\star},\alpha^{\star})\subset\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)
for
i\in V
.
We state the convergence rate of Algorithm
2
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
–
4
and
6
–
7
hold.
Let

m^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}\sigma_{\min}^{2}({\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)})
,
\eta_{B_{0}}=\{\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sigma_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{A}^{m(0)})\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{m}\|_{2}\}^{-1}
,

N=O(\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\kappa_{m}^{2}(k_{m}s^{\star}+\log M+\log p))
and
\pi_{0}=C_{\vartheta}\left\{1-{\sigma_{\min}^{2}(\mathbf{A}^{m^{\prime}(0)})\eta_{B_{0}}\nu_{x}}/2\right\}
,
where
C_{\vartheta}
,
C_{\gamma}
and
C_{\delta,i}

are constants whose explicit values are given in Appendix
C
.
Then, for each
1\leq i\leq p
, after
L
iterations of Algorithm
2
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(L)}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}\leq\pi_{0}^{L}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}+\frac{C_{\gamma}C_{\vartheta}}{1-\pi_{0}}\frac{\eta_{B_{0}}}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}+\frac{C_{\delta,i}C_{\vartheta}\eta_{B_{0}}}{1-\pi_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\star}s^{\star}k^{2}}{N}},

with probability at least
1-3\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\exp(-k_{m}s^{\star})
.

Remark 2
.

The quality of the initial guess of
\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}
depends on
M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}
. While
s^{\star}k^{2}
in the second term seems large at first glance, it is worth pointing out that the maximum degree of a node in functional graphical model scales with
s^{\star}k^{2}\ll k^{2}p
.

5.2
Convergence Analysis of Algorithm
1

We show the minimum number of iterations required for Algorithm
1
to produce a useful result. Additionally, Algorithm
1
converges in a linear rate under mild assumptions.

The following assumption constrains the space of the local region where Algorithm
1
takes place. The condition is further fulfilled by Theorem
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
.

Assumption 8
.

There exist constants
C_{A}
and
C_{B}
such that the initial guesses satisfy
\|\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{2}\leq C_{A}\|\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{2}
,
\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|\leq C_{B}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|
for any unitarily invariant norm and
\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)\top}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star\top}\|_{1}\leq C_{B}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star\top}\|_{1}

for
m=1,\ldots,M
and
i\in V
.

Let
C_{\alpha},C_{\beta}
,
C_{\eta_{A}},C_{\eta_{B}}
be constants depending on the data, as detailed in Appendix
C
.
We introduce the statistical error
\Xi:=\sqrt{N^{-1}(\Xi_{1}+\Xi_{2})}
with

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}:=C_{\alpha}\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}{k(k_{m}+k+\log M)},\quad\Xi_{2}:=C_{\beta}{s^{\star}(k^{2}+\log p)},

where
\Xi_{1}
is induced by the statistical error of estimating
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
and
\Xi_{2}
is by the statistical error of estimating
\mathbf{B}_{i}
.
Under Assumption
8
, we define
R_{0}
as the smallest positive real number satisfying

\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\|\mathbf{A}^{m(0)}-{\mathbf{A}^{m}}\|_{F}^{2}+\max_{i\in V}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(0)}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}\leq R_{0}^{2},
and

\displaystyle\delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left[-\{(s^{\star}+1)\min_{m=1,\ldots,M}k_{m}\wedge(\alpha k)^{2}\}\right],

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle:=\left[4\left\{1-p(s^{\star}+1)C_{\eta_{A}}\eta_{A}\right\}\vee C_{\vartheta}\left(1-MC_{\eta_{B}}\eta_{B}\right)\right].

We now state the convergence guarantee for Algorithm
1
, with proof in Appendix
D.4
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
–
4
,
6
–
8
hold and that,
\Xi^{2}\leq C_{1}(C_{A}^{2}\rho_{a}+C_{B}^{2}\rho_{b})
, and
N=O(\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\kappa_{m}^{2}\{k_{m}(s^{\star}+1)+\log p+\log M\})
.
The step sizes
\eta_{A}
and
\eta_{B}
are selected to satisfy
(i)
\eta_{A}\leq{C_{2}}\{p(s^{\star}+1)\}^{-1}
and

\eta_{B}\leq{C_{3}}{M}^{-1}
,
(ii)
p\eta_{A}=C_{\vartheta}M\eta_{B}
, and (iii)
\pi<1
,
where
\{C_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}
are positive constants.
After
L
iterations of Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\|\mathbf{A}^{m(L)}-{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}+\max_{i\in V}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}^{(L)}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\pi^{L}R_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{1-\pi}\Xi^{2},

with probability at least
1-10\delta_{0}
.

Remark 3
.

From the theorem it follows that Algorithm
1
converges to a local optimum that is close to the population parameters with distance of up to
2(1-\pi)^{-1}\Xi^{2}
if the number of iterations
L
exceeds
o((-\log(1-\pi)+2\log\Xi-2\log R_{0})/\log\pi)
.

5.3
Graph Recovery

The final step is to combine Theorem
5.1
,
5.3
, and Lemma
5.2
together and choose the appropriate parameters and the number of iterations.
We have the following main result.

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
7
hold and that
N
,
\eta_{A}
,
\eta_{B}
,
\eta_{B_{0}}
satisfy the conditions stated in Theorem
5.3
and Lemma
5.2
. Additionally,
N
is large enough so that
\Xi^{2}\leq(1-\pi)2^{-3}\Lambda^{2}(k)
and there exists a
k_{m}
such that
|\!|\!|\mathscr{L}^{m,r}|\!|\!|=o(1/\sqrt{k_{m}})
.
We set
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}^{(0)}
as (
4.3
), run Algorithm
2
with the number of iterations
-C_{1}\log\{(k^{2}+\log s^{\star})/{N}\}
and then run Algorithm
1
with
(-\log(1-\pi)+2\log\Xi-2\log R_{0})/\log\pi
iterations.
Let
\widehat{\mathscr{N}}_{i}
be the estimated edges based on the outcomes from Algorithm
1
with the edge selection threshold
\epsilon_{0}=2^{-1}\Lambda(k)
defined in (
4.1
). Then, we have

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\mathscr{N}}_{i}=\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star};1\leq i\leq p)\geq 1-14\delta_{0}.

Remark 4
.

The theorem states that we can recover the edge set
\mathscr{N}_{i}^{\star}
with probability at least
1-14\delta_{0}
. Note that to establish graph recovery with a reasonable sample size, the truncated latent signal
\Lambda(k)
cannot be too small.
As the minimum sample size scales with
\Lambda^{-2}(k)
, a sufficiently large sample size is required to meet the condition
\Xi^{2}\leq(1-\pi)2^{-3}\Lambda^{2}(k)
.

6
Related Work

We review relevant research on multimodal estimation and functional graphical models.

The low-rank latent space assumption has been widely explored in multimodal estimation
(
Zhou et al. 2015
;
Yang and Michailidis 2016
)
.
JIVE
(
Lock et al. 2013
)
jointly estimates shared low-rank components and individual low-rank components and has been successfully applied to biomedical data
(
O’Connell and Lock 2016
)
. Our work differs in the way that we find the low-rank model of the original data generation process, while previous work finds a low-dimensional representation of the data, providing a better interpretation of the underlying mechanism.
Multimodal integration framework has been widely used in joint prediction tasks.
Li et al. 2018
constructed linear additive low-rank predictors for joint multitask regression.
Li and Li 2022
proposed a statistical inference procedure to select significant modalities for integrative linear regression models. Another methodology is to stack multimodal data in a tensor and perform low-rank tensor regression
(
Zhou et al. 2013
)
. The predictive power of various models was improved by using rich multimodal data in applications, including clinical diagnoses
(
Wolfers et al. 2015
)
and biomarker detection
(
Mimitou et al. 2021
)
.

There is increasing interest in functional graphical models.
Qiao et al. 2019
established the penalized maximum log-likelihood framework for Gaussian functional data.
Qiao et al. 2020
successively extended it to the discrete sampling setting and further considered estimation of time-varying graphs.
Zapata et al. 2021
developed a separability condition for the multivariate covariance operator and applied it to learning functional graphical models.
Zhao et al. 2021
developed a function-on-function regression model, as the functional data version of the neighborhood regression method. While the aforementioned work focuses on the Gaussian distribution, another active line of work develops functional graphical model estimators under the non-Gaussian setting.
Li and Solea 2018
and
Lee et al. 2022
used additive conditional independence
(
Li et al. 2014
)
and proposed nonparametric estimators.
Moysidis and Li 2021
studied the joint estimation of multiple functional Gaussian graphical models by building a hierarchical structure on inverse covariance. Although this procedure is most closely related to our work, there are considerable differences in both methodology and theory.
Moysidis and Li 2021
extended the approach of
Guo et al. 2011
to the functional data setting. They imposed a hierarchical structure on the inverse covariance, and jointly estimated multiple graphs. Our method, in contrast, takes a generative perspective and studies a “single” latent graph shared over multiple views.

7
Simulations

The simulations focus on comparisons of sparse precision matrices, where we test four types of sparse graphs. First, we compare our models with three existing methods by computing the Area under the ROC Curve (AUC). Second, we compute the convergence distance with respect to the sample size, verifying the results from Theorem
5.3
. Details of data generation processes are presented in Appendix
K.1
, where we synthesize four types of different graph structures and two noise models.

7.1
Comparison

Figure 2:
The ROC curves of Graph 1–4. The additive noise is generated from noise
model 2. The AUC is discussed in Table
3
. The proposed method has consistent performance across four graphs and
p=\{50,100,150\}
.

Figure 3:
Distance v.s. statistical error. The solid lines denote the proposed algorithm. The dashed lines denote the case where
{\mathbf{A}^{m}}
is first initiated with true
\mathbf{A}^{m\star}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
and then refined by Algorithm
1
–
2
subsequently.

We compare the model with 3 other methods: FGGM
(
Qiao et al. 2019
)
, PSFGGM
(
Zapata et al. 2021
)
, FPCA
(
Zhao et al. 2021
)
, and JFGGM
(
Moysidis and Li 2021
)
. Since those candidates were not originally designed to learn latent graphs, we apply them to learn the graph of each modality separately and then take the intersection of the graphs as the surrogate of the latent graph. Let
\widehat{E}^{1},\widehat{E}^{2}
be the edge set estimated by one of the algorithms, then we construct the latent graph as
\widehat{E}^{z}=\{(i,j):(i,j)\in\widehat{E}^{1},(i,j)\in\widehat{E}^{2}\}
.
We fix
k
,
k_{m}
to be the true values and set
N=100
,
{\mathcal{T}}=[0,1]
and
p=\{50,100,150\}
. Since JFGGM requires computing an eigen-decomposition at each iterate, which is computationally expensive when
p
is large, we omit testing JFGGM under the setting of
p=150
.

To evaluate the performance of competing methods, we vary the sparsity parameters and plot the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curves, where the x-axis denotes the
False Positive Rate (FPR) of the estimated edges and the y-axis denotes the True
Positive Rate (TPR) of the estimated edges. Under the “AND” operation of edge selection and
i\neq j
, the TPR and FPR are defined respectively as:

\displaystyle\text{TPR}=\frac{|\{(i,j):(j,i),(i,j)\in E^{z}\cap\widehat{E}^{z}\}|}{|\{(i,j):(i,j)\in E^{z}\}|},\quad\text{FPR}=\frac{|\{(i,j):(i,j),(j,i)\in\widehat{E}^{z}\backslash E^{z}\}|}{|\{(i,j):(i,j)\not\in E^{z}\}|}.

To plot the ROC curve, we vary the sparsity level
s
for the proposed method and vary the sparse regularization coefficient
\lambda
for the competing methods. Each point in the ROC plot is
(\text{FPR}(s),\text{TPR}(s))
or
(\text{FPR}(\lambda),\text{TPR}(\lambda))
for
1\leq s\leq p
and
\lambda
is chosen from a grid of values
[0,\lambda_{\max}]
, where
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\neq j}N^{-1}\|{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{m}}({\mathbf{Y}_{j}^{m}})^{\top}\|_{F}
is sufficient as discussed in Proposition 1 in
Zhao et al. 2021
. Furthermore, we test the proposed method with step size
\eta_{A}
,
\eta_{B}
, and
\eta_{B_{0}}
chosen from
\{0.1,0.01,0.001,0.0001\}
and select the combination of step size
(\eta_{A},\eta_{B},\eta_{B_{0}})
that has the largest AUC. We run the simulation for
10
runs and take the average of the results. The simulation result is plotted in Figure
2
and the corresponding AUC is presented in Table
3
in Appendix
K.3
. The proposed method achieves the best or comparable performance for Graph 1–4.
Appendix
K
includes more simulation results along with discussion on the comparing methods.

7.2
Distance v.s. sample size

Empirically, we demonstrate that the metric
\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}^{2}+\max_{i\in V}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}
at convergence scales almost linearly with respect to
(k\sum_{m=1}^{M}k_{m}\vee k^{2}\log p)/N
, which matches the result of Theorem
5.3
.
2
2

2

We assume that
s^{\star}
is a constant here.
We test the graph model 1–3 with the magnitude of the off-diagonal entries further scaled down by half. For additive noise, we use noise model 1. For each simulation, we fix
k
,
p
, and
k_{m}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
and vary
N
.
We take the average of the results for
20
runs of simulations. From Figure
3
, we see that when
(k\sum_{m=1}^{M}k_{m}\vee k^{2}\log p)/N
is smaller than
0.45
,
\sum_{m=1}^{M}\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}
and
\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\|{\mathbf{A}^{m}}-\mathbf{A}^{m\star}\|_{F}^{2}+\max_{i\in V}\|\mathbf{B}_{i}-\mathbf{B}_{i}^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}
scale almost linearly with
(k\sum_{m=1}^{M}k_{m}\vee k^{2}\log p)/N

for most tasks. Note that the solid lines converge to the dashed lines as the x-axis approaches zero. This implies that the CCA initialization (
4.3
) is a consistent estimator, matching the result of Theorem
5.1
.

8
Brain Network Estimation with EEG-fMRI

We apply our proposed model to estimate the connectivity patterns of brain networks using concurrent measurements of EEG-fMRI
(
Morillon et al. 2010
;
Sadaghiani et al. 2010
)
. The dataset includes
23
test subjects in each of the two sessions. Both EEG and fMRI are first source-localized
(
Wirsich et al. 2020b
)
and then parcellated according to the Desikan-Killiany cortical atlas
(
Desikan et al. 2006
)
, which has
68
parcels. Each recorded session is
600
seconds. In the first
300
seconds, all subjects are synced to watch a short movie clip; in the following
300
seconds, all the subjects are resting. It is believed that brain networks display similar patterns across subjects during movie-watching as compared to the resting-state
(
Vanderwal et al. 2019
)
. Hence, we only use the first
300
seconds for estimation and testing. Our goal is to predict the brain
networks of session 2 using the data from session 1.
Specifically, we first learn the precision matrices using the data from session 1. Then, we apply the results to predict the brain network of session 2, where the log-likelihood is used for evaluation. The preprocessing pipeline is presented in Appendix
M.1
.

Since it is difficult to directly evaluate the prediction error of the latent graph, we design a surrogate task to demonstrate the effectiveness
of the proposed model. We used the estimated edge set
\widehat{E}^{z}
as auxiliary information to learn the graph of EEG and fMRI.
After obtaining the estimated edge set
\widehat{E}_{z}
through the proposed algorithm, we use it to estimate the precision matrices of fMRI and EEG. Let
m=1
denote the index for the fMRI modality and
m=2
denote the index for the EEG modality. Given an edge set
E
, we denote
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{E}(k_{m})
be the set of
pk_{m}\times pk_{m}
positive definite matrices with support associated to
E
:
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{E}(k_{m}):=\{{\bm{\Omega}}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{+}:\omega_{i,j}=0,\;(i,j)\not\in E\}
, where
\omega_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{m}\times k_{m}}
is the
(i,j)
-th submatrix of
{\bm{\Omega}}
. Let
\lambda\geq 0
. We estimate the graph by solving the problem:

\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}^{m}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{{\bm{\Omega}}^{m}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{E}(k_{m})}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{m}{\bm{\Omega}}^{m})-\log\det({\bm{\Omega}}^{m})+\lambda\sum_{i\neq j,(i,j)\in E}\|\omega_{i,j}^{m}\|_{1,1}.

(8.1)

When
\lambda=0
, this is a standard procedure to estimate the graph after obtaining an edge set through neighborhood regression
(
Ma and Michailidis 2016
)
. When
\lambda>0
, this means that the submatrix
\omega_{i,j}
is sparse, following a similarly structured assumption of the second condition of
\mathcal{K}_{B}(s,\alpha)
. We then use the skggm package
(
Laska and Narayan 2017
)
to optimize (
8.1
). The parameter
\lambda
is selected using 5-fold cross-validation with the BIC metric:
-2\widehat{L}_{N}+s\log N
, where
\widehat{L}_{N}
is the log-likelihood and
s
is the number of parameters. Let
\widehat{E}_{1}
,
\widehat{E}^{2}
be the edge sets obtained by running the regression type algorithm, as detailed in Appendix
M.2
, separately for fMRI and EEG data. We validate the effectiveness of
\widehat{E}^{z}
by testing three types of edge sets used in (
8.1
): individual edge set, latent edge set, and fused edge set. The individual edge set is
E=\widehat{E}^{1}
for the fMRI modality or
E=\widehat{E}^{2}
for the EEG modality; the latent edge set is
E=\widehat{E}^{z}
for both modalities; and the fused edge set is
E=\widehat{E}^{1}\cup\widehat{E}^{z}
for the fMRI modality or
E=\widehat{E}^{2}\cup\widehat{E}^{z}
for the EEG modality. Then, for the edge candidate and each data modality, we refit (
8.1
) to estimate the precision matrix.

To address the small sample size, we employ sampling with replacement and repeat the experiments for 5 runs. The result is shown in Table
1
, where we use the log-likelihood as the prediction score. The result indicates that, with the auxiliary edge information, the fused edge set has maximum in-sample log-likelihood and out-of-sample log-likelihood for both data modalities. Note that, with the latent edge set alone, we might neglect the individual graph structure for each modality, and hence the result is suboptimal.

9
Discussion

We have developed a new procedure for integrating multimodal functional data to estimate the underlying latent graph, providing a new statistical solution to answer scientific questions.

The functions are assumed to be continuous whereas we might only have access to the discrete samples in practice. We included a discussion to address this issue in Appendix
L
.

We studied the setting where data across modalities share the same set of nodes but have heterogeneous temporal characteristics.
In practice, many applications might have a different number of nodes across modalities. For example, the spatial resolution of the fMRI data is much higher than that of the EEG data. Therefore, fMRI is known to capture richer spatial information. On the contrary, the temporal resolution of the EEG data is much higher than that of the fMRI data, offering more expressive temporal information. It is left for future work to develop new statistical models and estimation procedures that accommodate more complex spatial and temporal discrepancies.

We thank Benjamin Morillon, Katia Lehongre, and Anne-Lise Giraud for their generosity of sharing the original EEG-fMRI raw data.
We thank Jonathan Wirsich for providing source-localized pre-processed EEG-fMRI data.
We thank Percy Zhai for sharing the code of the implementation of the functional Gaussian graphical model
(
Qiao et al. 2019
)
and functional neighborhood regression
(
Zhao et al. 2021
)
. The research project is funded by the National Science Foundation (NSF) Graduate Research Fellowships Program, NSF 2046795, 1909577, 1934986, 2216912, and NIFA award 2020-67021-32799.

Appendix A
Conditional Covariance Operator and Partial Covariance Operator

This section shows the analysis of Section
2.3
. Appendix
A.1
introduces the notation. The main result is presented in in Appendix
A.2
. We start by discussing the univariate case and the extension to multivariate case follows similarly. Appendix
A.3
shows the proof of Theorem
2.2
; Appendix
A.5
shows the proof of Corollary
2.3
; Appendix
A.6
presents the proofs of auxiliary lemmas.

A.1
Notation

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathscr{B}(\cdot)
be the Borel set. Define
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
as the space of square-integrable random element endowed with inner product
\mathbb{E}[\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}]
. Let
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
be the space of bounded linear operator from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{H}
. Since
\mathbb{H}
is a complete normed space, it follows that
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
is a Banach space. Given a linear operator
\mathscr{T}
, we say that
\mathscr{T}
is in the equivalence class of a zero operator if
|\!|\!|\mathscr{T}|\!|\!|=0
.

A.2
Main Results

In this section, we connect the relation of the partial covariance operator, defined later in (
A.3
) with the covariance operator defined in
Fukumizu et al. 2007b
. We first start with the simple case that discusses conditioning on the single random element. Consider
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
be mean-squared integrable random elements in a separable Hilbert space.
Let
\mathscr{K}_{12}
be the covariance operator for
\mathcal{Z}_{1}

\mathcal{Z}_{2}
and
\mathscr{K}_{1}
,
\mathscr{K}_{1}
be the variance operator of
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
respectively. It is known
(
Baker 1973
)
that there exists a unique bounded linear operator
\mathscr{V}_{12}
with norm
|\!|\!|\mathscr{V}_{12}|\!|\!|\leq 1
such that
\mathscr{K}_{12}=\mathscr{K}_{1}^{1/2}\mathscr{V}_{12}\mathscr{K}_{2}^{1/2}
,
\Image(\mathscr{V}_{12})\subset\overline{\Image(\mathscr{K}_{1})}
and
\ker(\mathscr{V}_{12})^{\perp}\subset\overline{\Image(\mathscr{K}_{2})}
. Then the conditional covariance operator is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}_{12\mid 3}=\mathscr{V}_{12}-\mathscr{V}_{13}\mathscr{V}_{32},

(A.1)

which could be applied to measure conditional inference.

Fukumizu et al. 2004
shows that
\mathscr{V}_{12\mid 3}=0
if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{2}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{3}
when
\mathbb{H}
is a RKHS. We will show that this property also holds under the Gaussian assumption in any separable Hilbert space.
Before establishing the theoretical results, we introduce the definition of the Gaussian random element. First, we introduce the definition of Gaussian linear space.

Definition A.1

(Gaussian linear space, adopted from Definition 1.2 in
Janson 1997
)
.

A Gaussian linear space is a real linear space of random
variables, defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
, such that each variable
in the space is centered Gaussian.

Furthermore, by Theorem 1.5 in
Janson 1997
, we know that any set of random variables in linear Gaussian space is jointly normal.
Next, we introduce the definition of Gaussian random element.

Definition A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
be a centered random element taking values in
(\mathbb{H},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{H}))
, we say
\mathcal{Z}
is a Gaussian random element if
\{\langle{\mathcal{Z}},{f}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}:f\in\mathbb{H}\}
forms a Gaussian linear space.

Consider three centered Gaussian random elements
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
for
i=1,2,3
taking values in
\mathbb{H}
. Our goal is to measure the conditional independence of
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
given the random element
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
.
Let
L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma(\mathcal{Z}_{3}),\mu)
be a closed linear subspace of
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
, where
\sigma(\mathcal{Z}_{3})
denote the smallest
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
.
Let
\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
be a bounded linear operator, where
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
is a Banach space. Since
\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}
is continuous, it is measurable and hence

\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}\mathcal{Z}_{3}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma(\mathcal{Z}_{3}),\mu)
. Additionally,
\mathcal{Z}_{i}-\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}\mathcal{Z}_{3}
is the residual of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
after regressing on
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
for
i=1,2
. The regression procedure is defined as:

\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{Z}_{i}-\beta\mathcal{Z}_{3}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\right],\quad i=1,2.

(A.2)

Since
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
are centered Gaussian random elements, the mean element of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}-\widetilde{\beta}_{i3}\mathcal{Z}_{3}
is a zero element of
\mathbb{H}
for
i=1,2
. The partial cross covariance operator
\mathscr{K}_{12\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
is defined as

\langle{\mathscr{K}_{12\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}f},{g}\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle{\mathcal{Z}_{1}-\widetilde{\beta}_{13}\mathcal{Z}_{3}},{g}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle{\mathcal{Z}_{2}-\widetilde{\beta}_{23}\mathcal{Z}_{3}},{f}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right],\quad f,g\in\mathbb{H},

(A.3)

which are bounded linear operators, as shown in Lemma
A.7
. We define the partial cross covariance operator
\mathscr{K}_{21\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
as the adjoint operator of
\mathscr{K}_{12\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
.
We establish the following properties.

Theorem A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
for
i=1,2,3
be centered Gaussian random elements taking values in
(\mathbb{H},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{H}))
.
\mathscr{K}_{12\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
and
\mathscr{K}_{21\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
are defined in (
A.3
). The following properties hold.

1.

\mathscr{K}_{12\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
and
\mathscr{K}_{21\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
are in the equivalence class of the zero operator if and only if
\mathscr{V}_{12\mid 3}
and
\mathscr{V}_{21\mid 3}
are in the equivalence class of the zero operator.

2.

\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
, denoted as
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{Z}_{2}\mid\mathcal{Z}_{3}
, if and only if
\mathscr{V}_{12\mid 3}
and
\mathscr{V}_{21\mid 3}
are in the equivalence class of zero operator.

To evaluate the conditional independence of two Gaussian random elements in the Hilbert space, one could check the partial covariance operator, realized by computing (
A.2
).
Theorem
A.3
shows the conditional independence properties when
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
are random elements.
Oftentimes, we encounter the situation when
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
is not a single element, but a set of elements. For instance, this is the case for estimating Gaussian graphical models. Let
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{p})^{\top}\in\mathbb{H}^{p}
be centered Gaussian random vector with
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
for
i=1,\ldots,p
. We could easily generalize Theorem
A.3
to a
p
-dimensional centered random vector by studying the linear subspace
L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma(\mathcal{Z}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,p),\mu)
. The result is stated in Theorem
2.2
.

A.3
Proof of Theorem
A.3

It suffices to verify the properties of
\mathscr{K}_{12,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
, and similar properties hold for
\mathscr{K}_{21,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\bullet$}}}}3}
. We begin with showing the first statement. Notice that since
\Image(\mathscr{V}_{ij})\subset\overline{\Image(\mathscr{K}_{i})}
and
\ker(\mathscr{V}_{ij})^{\perp}\subset\overline{\Image(\mathscr{K}_{j})}
for
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
,
\mathscr{V}_{12\mid 3}=0
if and only if
